Goose bumps produce little if any benefit to humans because our shoulder and arm hairs are short and usually covered by clothing.","paragraphIndex":20053,"printedPages":[111]},{"label":"Important detail","explanation":"In most other mammals, however, erected hairs make a frightened animal look larger and more intimidating (Figure 4.6).","paragraphIndex":20054,"printedPages":[111]},{"label":"How it works","explanation":"Here is another example of an evolutionary explanation, this time relating to old age: Throughout the animal kingdom, and for plants as well, life does not outlast the capacity to reproduce.","paragraphIndex":20055,"printedPages":[112]},{"label":"Living after you stop reproducing","explanation":"Living after you stop reproducing is disadvantageous from an evolutionary standpoint, because you would be competing with your offspring for food and shelter. Group selection works well for humans, because of our ability to punish or expel uncooperative people.","paragraphIndex":20056,"printedPages":[112,113]},{"label":"Important implication","explanation":"At its best, evolutionary psychology leads to research that helps us understand a behavior. The search for a functional explanation directs researchers to explore species’ different habitats and ways of life until we understand why they behave differently.","paragraphIndex":20057,"printedPages":[113]},{"label":"Research evidence","explanation":"The approach is criticized when its practitioners propose speculative explanations without supporting evidence.","paragraphIndex":20058,"printedPages":[113]}],"status":"whole_book_source_structured_classroom_notes","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"source_heading_facts","sourceFactCount":11,"editorialVerification":"source-aligned, individual paraphrase review pending","followUpExplanation":"The assumption is that any behavior characteristic of a species must have provided an advantage, at least in ancestral times.","followUpParagraphIndex":20052},{"headingId":"bp14-m4-1-h11","title":"In Closing: Genes and Behavior","level":1,"parentId":null,"printedPage":114,"pdfPage":132,"explanation":"Many other behaviors are influenced by genes but also subject to strong influence by experience.","paragraphIndex":20059,"bullets":[{"label":"Research evidence","explanation":"Studying the genetics of behavior is more difficult with humans than with laboratory animals, because researchers have such limited control over environmental influences and no control over who mates with whom. Inferring how human behavior evolved is also difficult, because we do not know enough about the lives of our ancient ancestors.","paragraphIndex":20061,"printedPages":[114]},{"label":"Underlying mechanism","explanation":"Certain genes influence the probability of developing Alzheimer’s disease or other conditions that occur mostly in old age.","paragraphIndex":20062,"printedPages":[115]}],"status":"whole_book_source_structured_classroom_notes","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"source_heading_facts","sourceFactCount":5,"editorialVerification":"source-aligned, individual paraphrase review pending","followUpExplanation":"Our genes and our evolution make it possible for humans to be what we are today, but they also give us the flexibility to change our behavior as circumstances warrant.","followUpParagraphIndex":20060}],"classroomParagraphs":["Mendel’s results demonstrated that inheritance occurs through genes, units of heredity that maintain their structural identity from one generation to another.","Most genes come in pairs because they are aligned along chromosomes (strands of genes) that come in pairs.","An exception to this rule is that a male mammal has unpaired X and Y chromosomes.","Classically, a gene has been defined as part of a chromosome, which is composed of the double-stranded molecule deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA). Sometimes a genetic outcome depends on parts of two or more chromosomes.","It’s an amazingly simple code, considering the complexity of body structures and functions that result from it.","Because each has one high taste sensitivity gene—let’s abbreviate it “T”—both parents readily taste PTC.","Therefore, a child in this family has a 25 percent chance of two T genes, a 50 percent chance of the heterozygous condition, and a 25 percent chance of being homozygous for the t gene. However, an example like this can be misleading, because it implies that a single gene produces a single outcome.","Researchers have identified at least 10 genes that contribute to variations in eye color, and at least 700 genes that contribute to differences in people’s height.","The genes on the sex chromosomes (designated X and Y in mammals) are known as sex-linked genes.","All other chromosomes are autosomal chromosomes, and their genes are known as autosomal genes.","In mammals, a female has two X chromosomes, whereas a male has an X and a Y. Psychological gender identification is another matter.) When biologists speak of sex-linked genes, they usually mean X-linked genes.","The small Y chromosome has a gene that causes the testes to form, but not as many genes as the X chromosome.","A man with the recessive form of this gene on his X chromosome is red-green color deficient because he has no other X chromosome.","Distinct from sex-linked genes are the sexlimited genes, present in both sexes but active mainly in one sex. Both sexes have the genes, but sex hormones activate them in one sex and not the other, or one sex more than the other.","Changing a DNA base to any of the other three types means that the mutant gene will code for a protein with a different amino acid at one location in the molecule.","Given that evolution has already had eons to select the best makeup of each gene, most mutations are harmful, except when the environment changes.","For example, a mutation in a gene controlling the immune system might provide better protection against a new virus. Because some mutations occur as an embryo is developing or later, what we call “identical” twins can have genetic differences.","Because microduplications provide new material for genetic change, they have been a necessary part of our evolutionary history.","For psychology, the important aspect of epigenetics is that experiences can produce epigenetic changes, altering gene expression in the brain.","Adding an acetyl group (COCH 3 ) to a histone tail loosens the histone’s grip on the DNA, facilitating the expression of the genes.","Removing an acetyl group tightens the histone’s grip on the DNA and silences the gene.","Another epigenetic change is to add a methyl group (CH 3 ) at the beginning of a gene, turning it off, or removing a methyl group to turn it on. Many experiences can alter the chemistry of the brain in a way that activates some genes and inactivates others.","Exposing a pregnant mouse to alcohol changes the brain epigenetics of her offspring.","If a mother rat is malnourished during pregnancy, her offspring alter the expression of certain genes to conserve energy and adjust to a world in which food will presumably be hard to find. In humans, drug addiction produces epigenetic changes in the brain.","Severe traumatic experiences in early childhood decrease methylation of many brain genes, increasing the later risk of depression or posttraumatic stress disorder.","If the variation depends largely on genetic differences, the characteristic has high heritability.","Heritability ranges from zero, indicating no genetic contribution to the variation, to one, indicating complete control.","Most people call monozygotic twins “identical,” a misleading term because the twins can differ, sometimes because of mutations that happened after conception.","However, the biological mother contributes not only her genes but also the prenatal environment. A heritability estimate shows how much something depends on variations in heredity within a population, and it varies from one population to another.","If some families have strict rules and others are lenient, the environmental influence is stronger, and therefore heritability will be lower.","Although a heritability estimate is interesting, it is more useful to know which genes are important for an outcome, and how those genes exert their effects. Because that approach tests a huge number of hypotheses at once (one for each gene), it has a risk of seeing an apparent effect by accident.","To avoid an accidental finding, the researchers need to set an unusually high standard for statistical significance, and therefore they need to test tens or hundreds of thousands of people.","A prime example is phenylketonuria (FEE-nil-KEET-uhn-YOOR-ee-uh), or PKU, a genetic inability to metabolize the amino acid phenylalanine.","If PKU is not treated, phenylalanine accumulates to toxic levels, impairing brain development and leaving a child cognitively impaired, restless, and irritable.","Although PKU is a hereditary condition, environmental interventions can modify it. Physicians in many countries routinely test the level of phenylalanine or its metabolites in each baby’s blood.","If a baby has high levels, indicating PKU, physicians advise the parents to put the baby on a strict low-phenylalanine diet to protect the brain.","People need to avoid meats, eggs, dairy products, grains, and especially aspartame (NutraSweet), which is 50 percent phenylalanine.","Physicians formerly believed that children with PKU could quit the diet after a few years. Even a genetically normal baby cannot handle the enormous amounts of phenylalanine that an affected mother might pass through the placenta or milk.","A gene can influence brain development, but it can influence behavior even without being expressed in the brain.","If their reactions influence your personality, then your genes altered your behavior by altering your environment.","Now imagine a child born with genes promoting greater than average height and running speed. Because of these factors, the child shows early success at basketball, and soon spends more and more time playing basketball.","As a result, the child spends less time than average on other pursuits—watching videos, playing chess, or anything else.","The point is that genes can influence behavior in roundabout ways. We should not be surprised that nearly every human behavior has some heritability.","Evolution is a change over generations in the frequencies of various genes in a population.","For example, because humans are more similar to chimpanzees and bonobos than to other species, biologists infer a common ancestor.","As new evidence becomes available, biologists often change their opinions about the evolutionary relationship between one species and another.","In contrast, the question of how species do evolve is a question of how the process works, and that process is a necessary outcome from what we know about reproduction. Because plant and animal breeders have long understood this idea, they choose individuals with a desired trait and make them the parents of the next generation through a process called artificial selection.","If certain individuals are more successful than others in finding food, escaping enemies, resisting illness, attracting mates, or protecting their offspring, then their genes will become more prevalent in later generations.","Let’s clarify the principles of evolution by addressing a few misconceptions:","Natural selection cannot favor a gene that benefits the species while disadvantaging the individuals with the gene. Some people have claimed that when lemming populations become too high, some of the lemmings jump off cliffs to decrease the overpopulation problem.","If that were true (and it is not!), the next generation would all descend from lemmings that failed to jump.","It emphasizes evolutionary and functional explanations— that is, how our genes reflect those of our ancestors and why natural selection might have favored genes that promote certain behaviors.","The assumption is that any behavior characteristic of a species must have provided an advantage, at least in ancestral times.","For example, people get “goose bumps”—erections of the hairs, especially on their arms and shoulders—when they are cold or frightened. Goose bumps produce little if any benefit to humans because our shoulder and arm hairs are short and usually covered by clothing.","In most other mammals, however, erected hairs make a frightened animal look larger and more intimidating (Figure 4.6).","Here is another example of an evolutionary explanation, this time relating to old age: Throughout the animal kingdom, and for plants as well, life does not outlast the capacity to reproduce.","Living after you stop reproducing is disadvantageous from an evolutionary standpoint, because you would be competing with your offspring for food and shelter. Group selection works well for humans, because of our ability to punish or expel uncooperative people.","At its best, evolutionary psychology leads to research that helps us understand a behavior. The search for a functional explanation directs researchers to explore species’ different habitats and ways of life until we understand why they behave differently.","The approach is criticized when its practitioners propose speculative explanations without supporting evidence.","Many other behaviors are influenced by genes but also subject to strong influence by experience.","Our genes and our evolution make it possible for humans to be what we are today, but they also give us the flexibility to change our behavior as circumstances warrant.","Studying the genetics of behavior is more difficult with humans than with laboratory animals, because researchers have such limited control over environmental influences and no control over who mates with whom. Inferring how human behavior evolved is also difficult, because we do not know enough about the lives of our ancient ancestors.","Certain genes influence the probability of developing Alzheimer’s disease or other conditions that occur mostly in old age."],"classroomCoverage":{"status":"source_structured_full_book","level":"comprehensive_book_derived","topicHeadings":11,"bullets":42,"newWords":1665,"allTopicsWithExplanations":true,"sourceScope":"verified_pdf_topic_facts_crosschecked_with_uploaded_markdown"},"classroomFigures":[{"id":"figure-4-2","headingId":"bp14-m4-1-h01","number":"4.2","title":"How Follow the book’s discussion from early neural growth to adolescence and old age, keeping structural change and behavioral change related but distinct."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m4-2-study-1","text":"The developing nervous system"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Early development creates neurons and supporting cells, then organizes them into pathways. Neurons migrate toward appropriate destinations, axons grow toward targets, and synapses form. The mature architecture is not built by genes acting alone; activity, chemical cues, competition, and experience all help refine it. Axons respond to guidance signals, and neurons that fail to make suitable connections may undergo programmed cell death. Neurotrophins and other signals influence survival. Some new neurons can appear later in life, though this should not be confused with the brain routinely replacing every damaged neuron."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m4-2-study-2","text":"Competition and fine-tuning"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Developing circuits commonly create more contacts than are ultimately retained. Inputs compete, and patterns of activity help stabilize some synapses while others weaken or disappear. The cortex differentiates into functional regions over time; sensory and social experience can shape connectivity and dendritic structure. This is why timing matters: the same environmental event may have different effects at different developmental stages. Sensitive periods describe times when experience has unusually strong or lasting influence, not absolute one-time opportunities for all learning."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m4-2-study-3","text":"Development throughout life"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The developing brain is especially vulnerable to certain insults, including inadequate nutrition, toxic exposures, and injury. Adolescence involves continued changes in neural organization and behavioral regulation. Aging brings additional alterations in function and structure, but age-related change is variable and does not mean that plasticity disappears. A lifelong view avoids treating the adult brain as finished or treating every difference in the young brain as pathology."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m4-2-study-4","text":"What studies of experience can show"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Changes in synapses, dendrites, and cortical responses can follow training or altered input. Such changes demonstrate that experience is physically represented in the nervous system. Yet greater dendritic branching or a larger regional response is not automatically 'better'; interpretation depends on the behavior and circuit. Neurons migrate toward appropriate destinations, axons grow toward targets, and synapses form. The mature architecture is not built by genes acting alone; activity, chemical cues, competition, and experience all help refine it. Axons respond to guidance signals, and neurons that fail to make suitable connections may undergo programmed cell death. Neurotrophins and other signals influence survival. The human central nervous system begins to form when the embryo is about 2 weeks old.","As the tube sinks under the surface of the skin, the forward end enlarges and differentiates into the","The fluid-filled cavity within the neural tube becomes the central canal of the spinal cord and the four ventricles of the brain, containing the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF).","The first muscle movements start at age 71⁄2 weeks, driven by spontaneous activity in the spinal cord, while the sensory organs are not yet functional. By the end of the first year, they weigh around 1,000 g, close to the adult average of 1,200 to 1,400 g.","Some cells remain where they are as stem cells, continuing to divide, whereas others migrate to other parts of the nervous system.","One of the major differences between human brains and chimpanzee brains is that human neurons continue proliferating longer.","Early in development, the primitive cells, not yet identifiable as neurons or glia, begin to migrate (move). A deficit in these chemicals leads to impaired migration, decreased brain size, and intellectual impairment.","The brain has many kinds of immunoglobulins and chemokines, reflecting the complexity of brain development.","Neurons develop different anatomies and functions, based on when and where they developed, and what genes they express.","Synaptogenesis, the formation of synapses, begins long before birth and continues throughout life, as neurons form new synapses and discard old ones. A slower stage of neuronal development is myelination, the process by which glia produce the insulating fatty sheaths that accelerate transmission in many vertebrate axons.","The traditional belief, dating back to Cajal’s work in the late 1800s, was that vertebrate brains formed all their neurons in embryological development or early infancy.","Beyond that point, neurons could modify their shape, but the brain could not develop new neurons.","However, researchers have discovered a few exceptions to the no-new-neurons rule.","In songbirds, one brain area that is necessary for singing loses neurons in fall and When researchers examined people’s skin cells, they found a concentration of 14 C corresponding to the year in which they did the test.","When researchers examined neurons in the cerebral cortex (of dead people at autopsy), they found a 14 C concentration corresponding to the year of the person’s birth.","These results indicate that the cerebral cortex forms no new neurons after the year of birth. In many mammals, new small interneurons form throughout life in the hippocampus, an important area for memory formation.","New neurons can be found in the human hippocampus up to about age 7, but beyond that age, some research methods have found evidence for new neurons and other methods have not.","If you asked someone to run a cable from one place to another in your room, your directions could be simple.","But imagine asking someone to run a cable to somewhere on the other side of the country. You would have to give detailed instructions about how to find the right city, the right building, and the location within the building.","The developing nervous system faces a similar challenge when it sends axons over great distances.","Biologist Paul Weiss conducted an experiment in which he grafted an extra leg to a salamander and then waited for axons to grow into it.","After the axons reached the muscles, the extra leg moved in synchrony with the normal leg next to it.","Today we compare the nervous system to computers, the most advanced technology of our era.) Later evidence supported the interpretation that Weiss had rejected: The salamander’s extra leg moved in synchrony with its neighbor because each axon found the correct muscle.","Roger Sperry, a former student of Weiss, performed a classic experiment that showed how sensory axons find their way to their correct targets. Sensory axons find their way in the same manner that axons to the muscles find their way.","Axons from other parts of the retina also grew back to their original targets.","Development proceeds by waves, as each type of neuron forms over a few days. Those that form earlier extend their axons longer and form more branches, thus making synapses with more targets.","Furthermore, neurons maturing at a particular time will make synapses with other neurons maturing at that time.","When axons reach their targets, chemical gradients steer them to approximately their correct location, but they cannot achieve perfect accuracy.","In fact, if a neuron cannot find its normal target (perhaps because that target has been damaged), in many cases it forms synapses onto some other target.","Each axon forms synapses onto many cells in approximately the correct location, and each target cell receives synapses from many axons. This adjustment depends on the pattern of input from incoming axons.","Consequently, axons from adjacent areas of the retina send almost simultaneous messages to the visual area of the thalamus.","In the development of the nervous system, we start with more neurons and synapses than we can keep, and then a selection process keeps the most successful combinations of synapses and rejects others. The principle of competition is an important one, although we should use the analogy with Darwinian evolution cautiously.","Each ganglion of the sympathetic nervous system has enough axons to supply all the muscles and glands in its area, with no axons left over.","Long ago, one hypothesis was that the muscles sent chemical messages to tell the sympathetic ganglion how many neurons to form.","She developed a love for research and eventually discovered that the muscles do not determine how many axons form. When any of its neurons forms a synapse onto a muscle, that muscle delivers a protein called nerve growth factor (NGF) that promotes the survival and growth of the axon.","It makes more neurons than necessary and discards the excess, thereby achieving a match between axons and targets.","In fact, all areas of the developing nervous system make more neurons than will survive into adulthood.","Nerve growth factor is a neurotrophin, meaning a chemical that promotes the survival and activity of neurons. (The word trophin derives from a Greek word for “nourishment.”) In addition to NGF, the nervous system responds to brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) and several other neurotrophins. Remember the term BDNF, because it becomes important again in the discussion of depression.","Early brain development is vulnerable to damage from malnutrition, toxic chemicals, and infections that would produce milder problems at later ages.","A fever is a brief annoyance to an adult, but it impairs neuron proliferation in a fetus.","Malnutrition decreases an adult’s pep, but before birth, it impairs brain development. Exposure to bisphenol-A (BPA), a chemical used in producing plastics, is hazardous at any age, but especially while the brain is developing.","Iron deficiency or impaired thyroid function produces lethargy in adults","Thyroid deficiency was common in the past because of iodine deficiency.","People who were born in the United States from about 1951 to 1980 were exposed to much lead because of the widespread use of leaded gasoline in cars. People who grew up in areas with high levels of lead had greater risks of intellectual loss and neurotic personality problems.","Even moderate drinking during pregnancy increases the risk of attention deficits, separation anxiety, and other problems. Alcohol inhibits receptors for glutamate, the brain’s main excitatory transmitter, and enhances receptors for GABA, the main inhibitory transmitter.","Because of the decrease in net excitation, many neurons undergo apoptosis.","It is like the speech of immigrant children: Those who enter a country when very young master the correct pronunciation, whereas older children retain an accent.","In one fascinating experiment, researchers explored what would happen to the immature auditory portions of the brain if they received input from the eyes instead of the ears.","On one side of the brain, researchers damaged the superior colliculus and the occipital cortex, the two main targets for the optic nerves.","Therefore, the optic nerve could not attach to its usual target, and the auditory area of the thalamus lacked its usual input. The result was this: What would have been the auditory thalamus and cortex reorganized, developing some (but not all) of the characteristic appearance of visual areas.","After the ferrets learned this task well, the researchers presented a light that the rewired side could see.","In short, the rewired temporal cortex, receiving input from the optic nerve, produced visual responses. In a related study with newborn mice, researchers damaged the thalamic nucleus responsible for touch.","The overall conclusion is that to some extent, the sensory input instructs the cortex about how to develop.","The blueprints for a house determine its overall plan, but because architects cannot anticipate every detail, construction workers have to improvise.","Because of the unpredictability of life, our brains have evolved the ability to remodel themselves in response to experience.","Hadley injected a dye that let them watch the structure of a living mouse neuron over days or weeks. They found that some dendritic branches extended between one viewing and another, whereas others retracted or disappeared (Figure 4.17).","The gain or loss of spines means a turnover of synapses, which relates to learning.","Decades ago, it was typical for a laboratory rat to live alone in a small gray cage.","Researchers called this an enriched environment, but it was enriched only by comparison to the deprived experience of a typical rat cage. A rat in the more stimulating environment developed a thicker cortex, more dendritic branching, and improved learning.","Hadley injected a dye that let them watch the structure of a living mouse neuron over days or weeks.","They found that some dendritic branches extended between one viewing and another, whereas others retracted or disappeared (Figure 4.17).","The gain or loss of spines means a turnover of synapses, which relates to learning.","Decades ago, it was typical for a laboratory rat to live alone in a small gray cage. Researchers called this an enriched environment, but it was enriched only by comparison to the deprived experience of a typical rat cage.","A rat in the more stimulating environment developed a thicker cortex, more dendritic branching, and improved learning.","As a result of this research, it has become more common to keep rats in a more enriched environment.","Physical activity is as beneficial for brain functioning in humans as in laboratory animals. But the more important question is how much we could boost intelligence beyond normal by providing special training or greatly enhanced experiences.","The psychological term is far transfer. ( Near transfer occurs if training on one task produces improvement on a similar task.) Far transfer is consistently a weak effect.","However, prolonged practice of a particular activity does produce definite brain changes that enhance the ability to perform the task.","Testing by asking them to point to the sound underestimates their skill, because blind people are not accustomed to pointing at anything.","Applying this procedure to the occipital cortex of people who are blind interferes with their ability to identify Braille symbols, whereas it does not affect touch perception in sighted people.","First, we have a good idea of where in the brain to look for changes—the brain areas responsible for hearing, finger control, and possibly vision (because it is important for reading music). The area devoted to the left fingers was largest in those who began their music practice early and therefore also continued for more years.","However, an alternative hypothesis is that people who were born with certain brain features are more likely than others to become musicians.","These results imply that at least some of the brain differences are a result of musical training.","However, it is also true that genetic differences predispose some people to better hearing. However, because the adults who started in childhood had practiced for more years, those studies did not separate the effects of starting early from the effects of total years of practice.","The evidence for visible brain changes after extensive practice is reasonably strong, but the evidence after brief practice leaves room for doubt.","Adolescents are widely regarded as impulsive and prone to seek immediate pleasure, as compared to adults.","Impulsiveness is a problem if it leads to risky driving, drinking, sex, spending sprees, and so forth.","In addition to being more impulsive than older adults, children and adolescents tend to “discount the future” more than adults do.","To be fair, the situation is not the same for people of different ages, because adults are more likely to be financially secure and better able to wait for a higher reward. Supreme Court to rule that the death penalty is unconstitutional for adolescents, because adolescents are less able to restrain their impulses.","However, although the prefrontal cortex is indeed not quite mature in adolescents, its immaturity is only a small part of the explanation for impulsivity.","Adolescents are widely regarded as impulsive and prone to seek immediate pleasure, as compared to adults.","Impulsiveness is a problem if it leads to risky driving, drinking, sex, spending sprees, and so forth.","In addition to being more impulsive than older adults, children and adolescents tend to “discount the future” more than adults do. To be fair, the situation is not the same for people of different ages, because adults are more likely to be financially secure and better able to wait for a higher reward.","Supreme Court to rule that the death penalty is unconstitutional for adolescents, because adolescents are less able to restrain their impulses.","However, although the prefrontal cortex is indeed not quite mature in adolescents, its immaturity is only a small part of the explanation for impulsivity. A likely explanation for risky adolescent behaviors is that the brain’s response to rewards, especially anticipation of rewards, increases during the teenage years.","Many neurons lose some of their synapses, and the remaining synapses change more slowly than before in response to experiences.","The thickness of the temporal cortex shrinks by about half a percent per year, on average.","The volume of the hippocampus also gradually declines, and certain aspects of memory decline in proportion to the loss of hippocampus. Distinguish biological recovery from learned behavioral compensation."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m4-3-study-1","text":"Immediate damage versus later consequences"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A brain injury can disrupt function directly through tissue damage and indirectly through swelling, inflammation, reduced blood flow, or changes in connected regions. Immediate treatment aims to limit further injury, while recovery over weeks and months involves different mechanisms. People can improve without returning to the exact pre-injury neural organization. It is therefore essential to distinguish anatomical repair from functional adaptation."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m4-3-study-2","text":"Several routes to recovery"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Diaschisis occurs when damage in one area depresses activity in connected regions; partial recovery may follow as those networks regain function. Surviving axons can sometimes sprout new branches, and other connections may strengthen. Denervation supersensitivity describes increased responsiveness after loss of normal input. True regrowth of long damaged axons in the adult central nervous system is limited, which helps explain why recovery is uneven. Training may support compensatory strategies even when lost tissue is not restored."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m4-3-study-3","text":"Cortical reorganization and phantom limbs"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Brain maps are not permanently fixed. When normal sensory input disappears, neighboring input can influence cortical regions that once responded to the missing body part. A person who has lost a limb may still experience sensations in it, including pain. Such phantom experiences show that body perception is generated by nervous-system representations rather than coming directly from current sensory input alone. Reorganization can be beneficial, but not every change automatically restores normal function."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m4-3-study-4","text":"Why rehabilitation is not a single technique"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Successful rehabilitation can involve physical practice, environmental supports, learning new routines, and addressing emotional or cognitive changes. The helpful intervention depends on the damage and the individual's remaining abilities. A sensible summary of plasticity is that neural circuits can adapt, but adaptation has biological constraints; clinical claims should be judged by controlled evidence and functional
